Language / Idioma: English | Português

Module navigation / Navegação do módulo: Part II: run prediction and outputs

1. Introduction

Prediction is the stage of the pipeline in which the available analytical objects are reorganized into a model-fitting framework. At this point, the goal is not yet to run the full prediction workflow. Instead, this module prepares the analytical structure that will later be used in the execution step.

The prediction problem studied here is not limited to fitting a single model. The project compares a reduced catalog of 18 models built from different combinations of environmental information (E), genomic information (G), phenomic information (P), weather-derived information (W), and their corresponding linear or kernel-based interaction structures. Because these models are intended to answer different prediction questions under different validation settings, the first step is to make their organization explicit and understandable before any expensive computation is performed.

This file therefore serves two complementary purposes. First, it prepares the inputs and computational settings needed for prediction. Second, it explains the analytical meaning of the model catalog and of the cross-validation schemes adopted in the study. In practical terms, this means that the reader should leave this module understanding what is being predicted, which sources of information are being compared, and how prediction quality will later be assessed.

Input objects, model catalog, and the role of this stage

This stage receives as input the objects available in output/Matrizes/. These include the phenotypic data table (Pheno), the incidence structures that connect observations to environments and weather, and the linear and nonlinear kernels derived from genomic and phenomic information. Together, these objects provide the full representation needed to define the prediction models.

The central object organized in this file is not a final result table. Instead, this stage documents the prediction framework: the computational settings, the catalog of 18 models, the corresponding Eta_list specifications, the validation design, and the naming convention that will later be used to store prediction results. In other words, this module prepares the prediction system used in the next stage.

This distinction is important for didactic reasons. Here we focus on understanding the structure of the prediction analysis: what enters each model, how the models differ, and what each validation scenario is designed to test. The execution itself is documented in the next module, where prediction files are inventoried and prepared for downstream summaries.

Inputs - Phenotypic observations and identifiers stored in Pheno - Environmental and weather design structures - Linear incidence-based relationships - Nonlinear genomic and phenomic kernels - Derived interaction kernels available for prediction

Organized objects - General computational settings for model fitting - The 18-model catalog used in the study - The full Eta_list structure that defines each prediction model - The cross-validation design used to evaluate prediction performance - The file-naming rules used downstream in execution, inventory, and visualization

Previous stage: variance_components.Rmd
Next stage: analysis_prediction_run_outputs.Rmd

2. Packages and options

Before defining any prediction model, it is useful to begin from a clean and reproducible working session. In a teaching-oriented script, this first step also has a practical function: it makes clear that model specification should not depend on objects left from previous runs.

At this stage, the emphasis is still on organization rather than execution. The prediction models are introduced, compared, and translated into their ETA structures, but the expensive fitting routine is left for the execution module. For that reason, the setup here is intentionally minimal. Scientific notation is turned off to improve readability of printed values, the workspace is cleared so that the objects used later in the file are those explicitly loaded from the available pipeline objects, and the parallel package is declared because the number of processor cores is already defined at this stage.

rm(list = ls())
options(scipen = 999)

library(parallel)
library(knitr)
library(kableExtra)

3. Prediction input objects

The prediction analysis starts from the matrices and kernels available in output/Matrizes/. This means that the current stage does not rebuild the data representation from scratch. Instead, it imports the saved objects that serve as the analytical foundation for model specification.

These objects represent different parts of the problem. Pheno contains the observation-level structure of the study, including traits, genotypes, and environments. ZG and ZP represent the linear genomic and phenomic relationships. ZE represents the categorical environment structure, whereas ZW carries the weather-derived information. The remaining objects correspond to interactions and nonlinear kernels that allow the comparison of simpler and more flexible prediction models.

Reading these objects directly from output/Matrizes/ is important for two reasons. First, it keeps the workflow reproducible by making prediction depend on the same stored objects used across the project. Second, it allows the reader to see how the prediction module is connected to the harmonization and kernel-construction step.

Nonlinear kernels are loaded separately for GY and KW using files with the corresponding suffixes. Linear, environmental, and weather matrices are shared between the two traits.

Pheno <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/Pheno.rds")

ZG   <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZG.rds")
ZP   <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZP.rds")
ZE   <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZE.rds")
ZW   <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZW.rds")

ZGZE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZGZE.rds")
ZPZE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZPZE.rds")
ZGZW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZGZW.rds")
ZPZW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/ZPZW.rds")

GGK <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGK_GY.rds")
GGK_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGK_KW.rds")
PGK <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGK_GY.rds")
PGK_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGK_KW.rds")
GAK <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAK_GY.rds")
GAK_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAK_KW.rds")
PAK <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAK_GY.rds")
PAK_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAK_KW.rds")

GGKE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGKE_GY.rds")
GGKE_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGKE_KW.rds")
PGKE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGKE_GY.rds")
PGKE_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGKE_KW.rds")
GAKE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAKE_GY.rds")
GAKE_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAKE_KW.rds")
PAKE <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAKE_GY.rds")
PAKE_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAKE_KW.rds")

GGKW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGKW_GY.rds")
GGKW_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GGKW_KW.rds")
PGKW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGKW_GY.rds")
PGKW_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PGKW_KW.rds")
GAKW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAKW_GY.rds")
GAKW_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/GAKW_KW.rds")
PAKW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAKW_GY.rds")
PAKW_KW <- readRDS("output/Matrizes/PAKW_KW.rds")

In the current version of the pipeline, the observation-level matrices available in output/Matrizes/ use the names ZE, ZGZE, ZPZE, ZGZW, and ZPZW to represent the categorical environment design and the interaction structures that link genomic and phenomic information to environment and weather. Keeping these names explicit here helps the reader connect the stored files to the prediction structures defined later in this module.

4. MCMC and general settings

Once the inputs are available, the next step is to define the computational settings that control model fitting and repeated validation. These values do not change the scientific question being asked, but they determine how the posterior distributions are explored and how stable the repeated prediction summaries can be.

The MCMC settings (nIter, burnIn, and thin) define how the Bayesian estimation is run. The number of repetitions (num_rep) and the number of folds (num_fold) define the ten random five-fold partitions. The experimental population is the same across repetitions, and each partition is shared across models and traits. The four held-out environments are specified in envs_leaveout; the partition seed is defined separately from the MCMC fitting seeds. The number of processor cores (n_cores) is recorded here because the execution module can use parallel computation when the expensive prediction stage is activated.

nIter <- 5000
burnIn <- 1000
thin <- 10
num_rep <- 10
num_fold <- 5
traits_to_run <- c("GY", "KW")
envs_leaveout <- c("CS11_WS", "CS11_WW", "CS12_WS", "CS12_WW")
partition_seed <- 20250829L

n_cores <- max(1, parallel::detectCores() - 1)

nIter
[1] 5000
burnIn
[1] 1000
thin
[1] 10
num_rep
[1] 10
num_fold
[1] 5
traits_to_run
[1] "GY" "KW"
envs_leaveout
[1] "CS11_WS" "CS11_WW" "CS12_WS" "CS12_WW"
partition_seed
[1] 20250829
n_cores
[1] 21

5. Model catalog

The prediction stage does not compare a single specification. Instead, it evaluates a reduced catalog of 18 models that represent different ways of combining environment (E), genomic information (G), phenomic information (P), weather-derived information (W), and their corresponding interaction structures. This catalog is useful because it allows the reader to see, in a single place, how the analytical comparison is organized before the models are fitted.

The table below should be read as a map of the modeling strategy adopted in the study. Each row identifies one model, gives it a stable manuscript label, and summarizes its main composition. The column Model uses the labels shown in tables and figures (M1 to M18), whereas EtaName keeps the computational identifier used internally by the prediction workflow (Eta1 to Eta18). Understanding this distinction makes it easier to connect the manuscript figures with the files and objects used during model fitting.

model_catalog <- data.frame(
  ModelNumber = 1:18,
  Model = paste0("M", 1:18),
  EtaName = paste0("Eta", 1:18),
  Description = c(
    "E + G + GE",
    "E + P + PE",
    "E + GGK + GGKE",
    "E + PGK + PGKE",
    "E + GAK + GAKE",
    "E + PAK + PAKE",
    "E + G + P + GE + PE",
    "E + GGK + PGK + GGKE + PGKE",
    "E + GAK + PAK + GAKE + PAKE",
    "E + W + G + GE + GW",
    "E + W + P + PE + PW",
    "E + W + G + P + GE + PE + GW + PW",
    "E + W + GGK + GGKE + GGKW",
    "E + W + PGK + PGKE + PGKW",
    "E + W + GGK + PGK + GGKE + PGKE + GGKW + PGKW",
    "E + W + GAK + GAKE + GAKW",
    "E + W + PAK + PAKE + PAKW",
    "E + W + GAK + PAK + GAKE + PAKE + GAKW + PAKW"
  ),
  FullEffects = c(
    "E + G + GE",
    "E + P + PE",
    "E + GGK + GGKE",
    "E + PGK + PGKE",
    "E + GAK + GAKE",
    "E + PAK + PAKE",
    "E + G + P + GE + PE",
    "E + GGK + PGK + GGKE + PGKE",
    "E + GAK + PAK + GAKE + PAKE",
    "E + W + G + GE + GW",
    "E + W + P + PE + PW",
    "E + W + G + P + GE + PE + GW + PW",
    "E + W + GGK + GGKE + GGKW",
    "E + W + PGK + PGKE + PGKW",
    "E + W + GGK + PGK + GGKE + PGKE + GGKW + PGKW",
    "E + W + GAK + GAKE + GAKW",
    "E + W + PAK + PAKE + PAKW",
    "E + W + GAK + PAK + GAKE + PAKE + GAKW + PAKW"
  ),
  InterpretiveDescription = c(
    "Linear genomic model with environment and G × E interaction",
    "Linear phenomic model with environment and P × E interaction",
    "Gaussian genomic model with environment and GGK × E interaction",
    "Gaussian phenomic model with environment and PGK × E interaction",
    "Arccosine genomic model with environment and GAK × E interaction",
    "Arccosine phenomic model with environment and PAK × E interaction",
    "Linear combined genomic + phenomic model with environment, GE, and PE",
    "Gaussian combined genomic + phenomic model with environment, GGKE, and PGKE",
    "Arccosine combined genomic + phenomic model with environment, GAKE, and PAKE",
    "Linear genomic-weather model with environment, weather, GE, and GW",
    "Linear phenomic-weather model with environment, weather, PE, and PW",
    "Linear combined genomic + phenomic + weather model with GE, PE, GW, and PW",
    "Gaussian genomic-weather model with environment, weather, GGKE, and GGKW",
    "Gaussian phenomic-weather model with environment, weather, PGKE, and PGKW",
    "Gaussian combined genomic + phenomic + weather model with GGKE, PGKE, GGKW, and PGKW",
    "Arccosine genomic-weather model with environment, weather, GAKE, and GAKW",
    "Arccosine phenomic-weather model with environment, weather, PAKE, and PAKW",
    "Arccosine combined genomic + phenomic + weather model with GAKE, PAKE, GAKW, and PAKW"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

model_catalog |>
  knitr::kable(
    caption = "Model catalog used in the prediction setup.",
    align = "ccclll",
    escape = FALSE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(

6. Model specification

Once the catalog is defined, the next step is to translate each model into the structure that will be passed to BGLR. In this workflow, that structure is stored in Eta_list. Each entry of Eta_list corresponds to one model from the catalog and records which effects or kernels will be used when the model is later fitted.

This part of the tutorial is important because it connects the biological meaning of the models to their computational implementation. The code below does not introduce a new scientific question; instead, it makes explicit how each analytical idea is encoded in a form that the prediction step can use. To keep this organization readable, the 18 models are presented in small conceptual groups.

6.1 Initialize the ETA container

The first step is simply to create the object that will hold all model specifications. Starting with an empty container makes it easier to add the models one group at a time and to keep the final structure aligned with the catalog presented above.

Eta_list <- list()

6.2 Models 1 to 6: single-source pathways with E

The first six models establish the basic comparison framework of the study. In all of them, the categorical effect of environment is retained, and this environmental baseline is then combined with one main source of biological information and its corresponding interaction with environment. These models are useful because they show how prediction behaves when genomic, phenomic, or kernel-based information is considered separately rather than in integrated form.

Eta_list[[1]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZG,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZGZE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[2]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZP,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZPZE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[3]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = GGK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = GGKE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[4]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = PGK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PGKE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[5]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = GAK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = GAKE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[6]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = PAK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PAKE, model = "RKHS"))

6.3 Models 7 to 9: integrated genomic and phenomic pathways without W

The next three models move from isolated information sources to combined pathways. Here the goal is to evaluate whether integrating genomic and phenomic information improves predictive performance before weather-derived information is introduced. Conceptually, this group helps the reader compare single-source models against multi-source models while keeping the weather component out of the equation.

Eta_list[[7]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZG,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZP,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZGZE, model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZPZE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[8]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = GGK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PGK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = GGKE, model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PGKE, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[9]]  <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = GAK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PAK,  model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = GAKE, model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = PAKE, model = "RKHS"))

6.4 Models 10 to 12: weather-augmented linear pathways

Models 10 to 12 introduce weather-derived information into the prediction structure. At this point, the comparison is no longer restricted to genomic and phenomic sources alone. The purpose of this group is to show how prediction changes when climatic similarity is incorporated together with linear genomic or phenomic relationships and their interactions.

Eta_list[[10]] <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZW,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZG,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZGZE, model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZGZW, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[11]] <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZW,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZP,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZPZE, model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZPZW, model = "RKHS"))

Eta_list[[12]] <- list(list(X = model.matrix(~ Pheno$Env - 1), model = "BRR"),
                       list(K = ZW,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZG,   model = "RKHS"),
                       list(K = ZP,   model = "RKHS"),

                       list(K = ZPZW, model = "RKHS"))

6.5 Models 13 to 18: weather-augmented nonlinear pathways

The final six models extend the previous comparison by combining weather-derived information with nonlinear kernel structures. These models are important because they represent the most flexible specifications in the catalog. In practical terms, they allow the workflow to compare simpler linear formulations against richer nonlinear alternatives that may capture more complex similarity patterns among genotypes, phenomic profiles, and environments.

6.6 Final integrity check of the ETA list

Before moving to the validation design, it is useful to verify that the computational representation of the models is complete. This final check confirms that the number of ETA objects matches the number of models listed in the catalog. Keeping these two structures aligned is essential, because later steps assume that model labels, model numbers, and ETA specifications refer to the same ordered set of 18 models.

The original list defines the GY models. For KW, the same model composition and component order are preserved, replacing only the nonlinear matrices with those calibrated for this trait. The Eta_by_trait object combines both lists so that model selection can follow the trait being analyzed.

7. Cross-validation design

Prediction accuracy depends not only on the fitted model, but also on the prediction problem being imposed. In this workflow, the four validation schemes are obtained from two shared-fit structures: within each repetition, one genotype-based fit is run for each of five folds and its predictions are evaluated as CV1 and CV2; for CV0 and CV00, one leave-one-environment-out fit is run for each environment × fold combination and the two targets are evaluated from that same fit. The execution module uses 10 repetitions, each containing the five-fold genotype partition. Because these scenarios are not equivalent, model performance must always be interpreted together with the exact masking rule used in the execution module.

This section clarifies what is hidden in each validation scheme and what scientific question each one is intended to answer. That distinction is essential before moving to the execution stage, because the same model may perform very differently depending on whether the challenge lies on the genotype axis, on the environment axis, or on both simultaneously.

Before defining the evaluation targets, we allocate unique Pedigree identifiers to five folds, once per repetition. The original random allocation is retained, with a seed depending only on the repetition. All observations of a genotype receive the same assignment, regardless of environment. All 18 models, GY, KW and the four scenarios use the same partition within a repetition. Each fit’s MCMC seed will be defined separately in Part II.

Pedigree <- unique(Pheno$Pedigree)

obs_per_fold <- ceiling(length(Pedigree) / num_fold)
fold_partitions <- vector("list", num_rep)

for (rep_num in seq_len(num_rep)) {
  set.seed(partition_seed + rep_num)

  folds <- sample(rep(
    seq_len(num_fold),
    each = obs_per_fold,
    length.out = length(Pedigree)
  ))

  fold_partitions[[rep_num]] <- data.frame(
    Pedigree = Pedigree,
    Rep = rep_num,
    Fold = folds,
    stringsAsFactors = FALSE
  )
}

fold_partitions <- do.call(rbind, fold_partitions)
rownames(fold_partitions) <- NULL

The table reports the number of genotypes per fold in each repetition. The number of observations actually evaluated in each environment may vary with phenotypic data availability.

fold_counts <- with(fold_partitions, table(Rep, Fold))

fold_counts_table <- data.frame(
  Rep = as.integer(rownames(fold_counts)),
  as.data.frame.matrix(fold_counts),
  Total = rowSums(fold_counts),
  check.names = FALSE,
  row.names = NULL
)

names(fold_counts_table)[seq_len(num_fold) + 1L] <-
  paste("Fold", seq_len(num_fold))

knitr::kable(
  fold_counts_table,
  caption = "Number of genotypes by repetition and fold."
) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(
    bootstrap_options = c("striped", "hover", "condensed", "responsive"),
    full_width = TRUE
  )
Number of genotypes by repetition and fold.
Rep Fold 1 Fold 2 Fold 3 Fold 4 Fold 5 Total
1 66 66 66 66 65 329

3 66 66 66 66 65 329
4 66 66 66 66 65 329

The complete assignment table will be written once before fitting in Part II and read by both execution flows (CV1/CV2 and CV0/CV00). The writing chunk below documents the format but is disabled during rendering. Thus, neither document overwrites partitions used in a completed run.

dir.create("output/results", recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)

write.csv(
  fold_partitions,
  "output/results/fold_partitions.csv",
  row.names = FALSE
)

7.1 Validation scenarios and their scientific meaning

The four validation schemes adopted in this study are summarized below. Each one corresponds to a different prediction target and therefore evaluates a different notion of generalization. Read this table as a map of the prediction questions that will later be implemented in the execution submodule.

cv_scenarios <- data.frame(
  CV = c("CV1", "CV2", "CV0", "CV00"),
  WhatIsMasked = c(
    "All observations from held-out genotypes",
    "No new masking; evaluation on training genotypes from the CV1 fit",
    "All observations from held-out genotypes and one held-out environment",
    "Same masking as CV0; evaluation of held-out genotypes in the held-out environment"
  ),
  MainQuestion = c(
    "Can the model predict completely new genotypes?",
    "How well does the same CV1 fit describe the genotypes that remained in training?",
    "Can the model predict previously seen genotypes in a completely held-out environment?",
    "Can the model predict new genotypes inside a completely held-out environment?"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

cv_scenarios |>
  knitr::kable(
    caption = "Cross-validation scenarios and their scientific meaning.",
    align = "ccc",
    escape = FALSE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(
    bootstrap_options = c("striped", "hover", "condensed", "responsive"),
    full_width = TRUE,
    position = "center",
    font_size = 13
  ) |>
  kableExtra::row_spec(0, bold = TRUE)
Cross-validation scenarios and their scientific meaning.
CV WhatIsMasked MainQuestion
CV1 All observations from held-out genotypes Can the model predict completely new genotypes?
CV2 No new masking; evaluation on training genotypes from the CV1 fit How well does the same CV1 fit describe the genotypes that remained in training?
CV0 All observations from held-out genotypes and one held-out environment Can the model predict previously seen genotypes in a completely held-out environment?
CV00 Same masking as CV0; evaluation of held-out genotypes in the held-out environment Can the model predict new genotypes inside a completely held-out environment?

7.2 Prediction when genotypes are partially or completely unobserved

The first two schemes focus on genotype-level uncertainty, but they are produced from the same fit within each fold. In CV1, all observations from the held-out genotypes are removed before fitting and the evaluation is performed on those omitted genotypes. In CV2, no additional masking is introduced; instead, the same fit is evaluated on the genotypes that remained in training. This difference matters because CV1 measures extrapolation to unseen genotypes, whereas CV2 measures in-sample recovery under exactly the same fold split. Each of the 10 repetitions contains five genotype folds, so five fold-level CV1/CV2 result sets are generated within each repetition before the downstream summaries report performance across the 10 repetitions.

cv12_design <- data.frame(
  Scenario = c("CV1", "CV2"),
  TrainingSet = c(
    "All observations except those from held-out genotypes",
    "Exactly the same fitted model used for CV1"
  ),
  EvaluationTarget = c(
    "Predictions for held-out genotypes",
    "Predictions for the genotypes that remained in training"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

cv12_design |>
  knitr::kable(
    caption = "Design logic for CV1 and CV2.",
    align = "ccc",
    escape = FALSE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(
    bootstrap_options = c("striped", "hover", "condensed", "responsive"),
    full_width = TRUE,
    position = "center",
    font_size = 13
  ) |>
  kableExtra::row_spec(0, bold = TRUE)
Design logic for CV1 and CV2.
Scenario TrainingSet EvaluationTarget
CV1 All observations except those from held-out genotypes Predictions for held-out genotypes
CV2 Exactly the same fitted model used for CV1 Predictions for the genotypes that remained in training

7.3 Prediction in environments that are not fully available during training

The schemes CV0 and CV00 shift the difficulty from the genotype axis to the environment axis, but they are also generated from the same fit within each environment × fold combination. First, the training response keeps only the genotypes assigned to the training portion of the fold. Then, an entire environment is masked and the model is refit under that leave-one-environment-out rule. CV0 evaluates the genotypes that were already seen in other environments, whereas CV00 evaluates the genotypes that were held out by the fold split. Within each of the 10 repetitions, the same five-fold genotype partition is reused across the four held-out environments. Thus, the fold-level files are intermediate validation outputs, whereas the final summaries describe performance across 10 repetitions. This distinction is essential because CV00 combines both genotype novelty and environment novelty.

cvloo_design <- data.frame(
  Scenario = c("CV0", "CV00"),
  TrainingSet = c(
    "Training genotypes only, with one environment fully masked",
    "Exactly the same fitted model used for CV0"
  ),
  EvaluationTarget = c(
    "Predictions for training genotypes inside the held-out environment",
    "Predictions for held-out genotypes inside the held-out environment"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

cvloo_design |>
  knitr::kable(
    caption = "Design logic for CV0 and CV00.",
    align = "ccc",
    escape = FALSE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(
    bootstrap_options = c("striped", "hover", "condensed", "responsive"),
    full_width = TRUE,
    position = "center",
    font_size = 13
  ) |>
  kableExtra::row_spec(0, bold = TRUE)
Design logic for CV0 and CV00.
Scenario TrainingSet EvaluationTarget
CV0 Training genotypes only, with one environment fully masked Predictions for training genotypes inside the held-out environment
CV00 Exactly the same fitted model used for CV0 Predictions for held-out genotypes inside the held-out environment

Assignment to the training set refers to the genotype partition. Actual response availability in the training environments depends on the observed data. Evaluation retains the original inclusion and masking rules; no new genotype filtering is imposed.

7.4 File naming and downstream reproducibility

The prediction workflow uses a hierarchical output layout so that each validation scenario, environment, trait, repetition, and fold can be inspected independently. Scenario-specific CSV files are written under output/results/. Persistent BGLR fitting artifacts are stored only for the fit-owning scenarios: under bglr_runs/CV1/ for the shared CV1/CV2 fit and under bglr_runs/CV0/ for the shared CV0/CV00 fit. Therefore, CV2 and CV00 have their own prediction-result CSV trees but do not have independent BGLR fit trees. In these computational paths, EtaName is the internal model identifier, whereas the manuscript-facing label is stored in Model. Keeping this distinction explicit prevents the result files from being confused with the shared fitting artifacts.

prediction_file_patterns <- data.frame(
  Scenario = c("CV1", "CV2", "CV0", "CV00"),
  ExampleCSV = c(
    "CV1/GY/rep02/fold03/CV1_GY_Eta1_rep02_fold03.csv",
    "CV2/KW/rep08/fold01/CV2_KW_Eta12_rep08_fold01.csv",
    "CV0/CS11_WS/GY/rep01/fold04/CV0_GY_CS11_WS_Eta10_rep01_fold04.csv",
    "CV00/CS12_WW/KW/rep10/fold02/CV00_KW_CS12_WW_Eta18_rep10_fold02.csv"
  ),
  SharedFitLocation = c(
    "bglr_runs/CV1/GY/rep02/fold03/Eta1/",
    "Shared with CV1: bglr_runs/CV1/KW/rep08/fold01/Eta12/",
    "bglr_runs/CV0/CS11_WS/GY/rep01/fold04/Eta10/",
    "Shared with CV0: bglr_runs/CV0/CS12_WW/KW/rep10/fold02/Eta18/"
  ),
  stringsAsFactors = FALSE
)

prediction_file_patterns |>
  knitr::kable(
    caption = "Examples of prediction file naming patterns used downstream.",
    align = "ccc",
    escape = FALSE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::kable_styling(
    bootstrap_options = c("striped", "hover", "condensed", "responsive"),
    full_width = TRUE,
    position = "center",
    font_size = 13,
    fixed_thead = TRUE
  ) |>
  kableExtra::row_spec(0, bold = TRUE) |>
  kableExtra::scroll_box(width = "100%", height = "360px")

CV2 CV2/KW/rep08/fold01/CV2_KW_Eta12_rep08_fold01.csv Shared with CV1: bglr_runs/CV1/KW/rep08/fold01/Eta12/

In addition to the existing aggregate CSVs, Part II will store individual predictions under output/results/individual_predictions/, organized by scenario and the same identification combinations. Observed and predicted values will be retained without presentation rounding. The following table defines the fields needed for metrics, plots and paired comparisons.

Column Description
ObsID Observation identifier
Pedigree Genotype identifier
Env Observation environment
Trait GY or KW
Model Manuscript model identifier (M1–M18)
Rep Repetition
Fold Evaluation fold
Env_leave Masked environment (NA for CV1/CV2)
Observed Observed phenotype
Predicted Posterior mean of fit$yHat
Training Whether the response was available to the fit
CV Evaluation scenario

7.5 Evaluation metrics and result summaries

Linear association between observed (\(y\)) and predicted (\(\hat y\)) values remains the primary metric, calculated using Pearson correlation. Spearman correlation and the Czekanowski coincidence index complement this assessment in terms of genotype ranking and selection, respectively.

\[r=\operatorname{cor}(y,\hat y),\qquad \rho_s=\operatorname{cor}(\operatorname{rank}(y),\operatorname{rank}(\hat y)).\]

For Spearman, ties receive average ranks. For Czekanowski, let \(S_y\) and \(S_{\hat y}\) be the sets of genotypes selected by the highest observed and predicted values, respectively:

\[CZ=\frac{2|S_y\cap S_{\hat y}|}{|S_y|+|S_{\hat y}|}.\]

The selection specification is a 20% intensity, with \(k=\lceil 0.20n\rceil\), where \(n\) is the number of eligible genotypes with valid pairs in the evaluation group. With both sets of size \(k\), the index is \(|S_y\cap S_{\hat y}|/k\). Ties at the selection boundary are resolved deterministically by ascending Pedigree identifier. This calculation assumes one observation per genotype in the evaluated environment; uniqueness must be verified before application.

All three metrics are calculated within each model, trait, scenario, repetition, fold and environment combination, using the same valid pairs of observed and predicted values. Undefined correlations are not converted to zero. The original Pearson calculation and aggregation are retained; the additional metrics follow the same summary hierarchy. Fold means are consolidated within each repetition, retaining environment-specific results and aggregation across environments where applicable. Error bars represent the standard deviation of the ten repetition means, not confidence intervals. For CV2, results describe in-sample fit.

7.6 Statistical comparison by paired bootstrap

The same genotypes are resampled with replacement for the models being compared, retaining their observed and predicted records across environments, folds and repetitions. The bootstrap assesses differences in performance conditional on the existing predictions, without additional BGLR fits. The proposed configuration is 5,000 resamples and a 95% confidence level. Intervals for differences between models are distinct from descriptive error bars across repetitions. Comparison families, multiplicity control and the treatment of repeated genotypes in Czekanowski during resampling will be specified at the results-analysis stage; the 50 repetition-fold combinations are not assumed to be independent.

8. Transition to the execution submodule

At this point, the prediction stage has been organized from three complementary perspectives: the analytical inputs are defined, the reduced catalog of 18 models is made explicit, and the validation schemes are linked to the scientific questions they are meant to answer. The execution stage will use the shared partitions, apply the original masking rules, store individual predictions and calculate all three metrics. Paired bootstrap comparisons will be performed in the subsequent results analysis.

The next submodule, analysis_prediction_run_outputs.Rmd, therefore moves from design to implementation. There the emphasis shifts from model definition and validation logic to execution records, persistent BGLR artifacts organized by scenario, and recursive inventories that make the prediction workflow easier to inspect and reuse in the downstream modules.

Stage navigation

This module connects the structural definition of the prediction analysis to the execution-and-outputs stage that follows. Readers who want to review how the model set was constructed should remain in this file. Readers who want to inspect how the predictions are executed and organized should continue to the next module.
